Natural history collection: A researcher arranges fallen leaves and pinecones on a wooden table. A magnifier reveals veins, paper bags and brushes sit nearby, and collecting tools protrude from a canvas bag.
Use an eye-level medium-wide view with a calm, observational mood.
Create one coherent environmental documentary photograph on an actual 1024 by 1024 square canvas. The person and the described activity are the main visual subject. Keep all people adults. Use only the principal action already described, rather than adding competing activities or a crowd. Arrange the named tools and objects on credible supporting surfaces, preserving their relationship to the task. Hands should meet handles or materials correctly, with natural anatomy and clothing folds that follow the pose. Leave sufficient space around the head, elbows and working tools so that the frame does not accidentally cut through important details.
Build a clear foreground, middle distance and background. Include only a few relevant foreground objects to guide the eye toward the activity. Preserve the complete silhouette of the central subject. Use a gentle reduction in contrast and moderate background softness to convey depth, while keeping the location recognizable. Align the perspective of the floor, work surfaces and architectural elements. Doorways, windows, shelves and supports should have plausible proportions. Avoid floating objects, duplicated tools, intersecting surfaces, impossible reflections and exaggerated wide angle distortion. Preserve the practical scale of every object relative to the person.
Use physically plausible light from a direction that can be understood from the scene. Illuminate the working hands and the most important material, with gradual transitions between light and shade. A small amount of reflected light may reveal detail in the shadows, but do not flatten the whole image. Keep bright surfaces within a natural exposure range. Any rim light should be subtle rather than a glowing outline. Make contact shadows visible beneath objects and where surfaces meet. Reflections on metal or glass must agree with the surrounding space and the direction of illumination.
Distinguish materials through texture rather than extreme contrast. Wood should retain its grain and minor wear, fabric should show its weave and believable folds, and paper should retain a soft matte surface. Where plants, stone, clay, water or food occur in the brief, preserve their particular physical character. Transparent surfaces should remain transparent, and polished objects should not resemble mirrors unless their material requires it. Avoid a uniform plastic appearance. Include restrained signs of ordinary use such as small scratches, creases, damp patches or irregular arrangements, without making the environment dirty or abandoned.
Keep a natural color palette drawn from the location and its materials. Reserve a limited accent color for a small tool or item of clothing rather than scattering bright colors throughout the composition. Skin tones should respond naturally to reflected environmental light. The image should feel carefully observed, with enough detail to inspect the activity and surrounding textures, but without halos from excessive sharpening. Do not add artificial lens flares, strong color filters, dramatic motion streaks, a collage, decorative borders or a separate inset image. Exclude readable lettering, subtitles, watermarks, brand marks and labels. The final picture should communicate a specific place, an understandable action and a consistent spatial arrangement at first glance.
Within this natural history collection, establish a clear visual route from the person's main action to the tool or material involved, then to the surroundings. Avoid background edges crossing the head or hands. Direct the person's gaze toward the task, with a focused but relaxed expression and a practical stance. Use ordinary working clothes suited to the environment, without theatrical costume or makeup. Place frequently used tools within reach, with spare materials farther away and a credible route through the working area. Treat this as a briefly observed moment in an ongoing activity, rather than a promotional pose directed at the camera.
